Vemurafenib: (Moderate) Concomitant use of vemurafenib and glimepiride may result in increased glimepiride concentrations. Vemurafenib is a CYP2C9 inhibitor and glimepiride is a CYP2C9 substrate. Monitor serum glucose concentrations if glimepiride is coadministered with CYP2C9 inhibitors. Dosage adjustments may be necessary.
Verteporfin: (Moderate) Use caution if coadministration of verteporfin with sulfonylureas is necessary due to the risk of increased photosensitivity. Verteporfin is a light-activated drug used in photodynamic therapy; all patients treated with verteporfin will be photosensitive. Concomitant use of other photosensitizing agents like sulfonylureas may increase the risk of a photosensitivity reaction.
Vitamin B Complex Supplements: (Moderate) Niacin (nicotinic acid) interferes with glucose metabolism and can result in hyperglycemia. Changes in glycemic control can usually be corrected through modification of hypoglycemic therapy. Monitor patients taking antidiabetic agents for changes in glycemic control if niacin (nicotinic acid) is added or deleted to the medication regimen. Dosage adjustments may be necessary.
Vonoprazan; Amoxicillin; Clarithromycin: (Moderate) The concomitant use of clarithromycin and antidiabetic agents can result in significant hypoglycemia. Careful monitoring of blood glucose is recommended. (Moderate) The concomitant use of clarithromycin and oral hypoglycemic agents can result in significant hypoglycemia. With certain hypoglycemic drugs such as the thiazolidinediones, inhibition of CYP3A enzyme by clarithromycin may be involved; however, CYP3A is not a major metabolism route for pioglitazone and rosiglitazone. Careful monitoring of glucose is recommended.
Voriconazole: (Moderate) Voriconazole should be used cautiously with sulfonylureas. The combination of voriconazole and oral antidiabetic agents may result in severe hypoglycemia. Voriconazole may inhibit the metabolism of sulfonylureas. Blood glucose concentrations should be monitored and possible dose adjustments of hypoglycemics may need to be made.
Warfarin: (Moderate) Monitor blood glucose and INR during concomitant use of sulfonylureas and warfarin. Concomitant use may increase the risk of hypoglycemia and produce an unpredictable effect on warfarin response, potentially increasing the risk of bleeding or reducing warfarin efficacy. These effects may be related to alterations in protein binding, although the exact mechanism of interaction is unknown.
Zafirlukast: (Moderate) Glimepiride is metabolized by CYP2C9. It is possible for serum concentrations of glimepiride to rise when coadministered with drugs that inhibit CYP2C9 like zafirlukast. Monitor serum glucose concentrations if glimepiride is coadministered with zafirlukast. Dosage adjustments may be necessary. (Moderate) It is possible that an increase in the exposure of pioglitazone may occur when coadministered with drugs that inhibit CYP2C8 such as montelukast and zafirlukast. Although montelukast or zafirlukast administered with pioglitazone in vivo did not significantly increase pioglitazone concentrations, patients should be monitored for changes in glycemic control if any of these CYP2C8 inhibitors are coadministered with pioglitazone.
Ziprasidone: (Moderate) Atypical antipsychotic therapy may aggravate diabetes mellitus and cause metabolic changes such as hyperglycemia. Monitor patients on antidiabetic agents for worsening glycemic control. Atypical antipsychotics have been associated with metabolic changes, including hyperglycemia, diabetic ketoacidosis, hyperosmolar, hyperglycemic states, and diabetic coma. Aggravation of diabetes mellitus has been reported. Possible mechanisms include atypical antipsychotic-induced insulin resistance or direct beta-cell inhibition. (Moderate) Monitor blood glucose during concomitant atypical antipsychotic and sulfonylurea use. Atypical antipsychotic therapy may aggravate diabetes mellitus. Atypical antipsychotics have been associated with metabolic changes, including hyperglycemia, diabetic ketoacidosis, hyperosmolar, hyperglycemic states, and diabetic coma. Possible mechanisms include atypical antipsychotic-induced insulin resistance or direct beta-cell inhibition.

Adverse Reactions
  Headline
  Adverse Reactions
  General Information
  NOTE: This monograph discusses the adverse effects associated with use of glimepiride; pioglitazone combination tablets. Clinicians may wish to consult the individual drug monographs for more information.Hypoglycemia, which can be severe, can occur during glimepiride; pioglitazone therapy. During clinical trials, the incidence of hypoglycemia with pioglitazone (30 or 45 mg/day) in combination with a sulfonylurea was 13.4—15.7%. Hypoglycemia symptoms include hunger, pallor, nausea, fatigue, diaphoresis, headache, palpitations, numbness of the mouth, tingling in the fingers, tremor, muscle weakness, visual changes, hypothermia, uncontrolled yawning, irritability, mental confusion, sinus tachycardia, shallow breathing, or loss of consciousness. Hypoglycemia can be a result of excessive dosage, but it also could be due to other factors such as improper diet or excessive physical activity. Prolonged hypoglycemia can result in irreversible brain damage and even death. Patients experiencing symptoms of hypoglycemia should be instructed to ingest 20 grams glucose; if symptoms do not resolve, this can be repeated in 15—20 minutes. In addition, patients should be instructed to ingest a full snack or meal shortly thereafter. For patients who are unconscious or unwilling, parenteral glucagon can be used. In the case of severe hypoglycemia, intravenous dextrose is the treatment of choice. In addition, sulfonylureas cross the placenta, and the newborns of women who have been receiving the drug have reportedly developed severe hypoglycemia.Edema (peripheral edema) has been reported more commonly in patients receiving pioglitazone (4.8%) compared to patients receiving placebo (1.2%), and may be dose related. Post-marketing cases of congestive heart failure have been reported in patients receiving pioglitazone (patients with and without a prior history of heart disease). Furthermore, in a 24-week post-marketing safety study, pioglitazone (n = 262) was compared to glyburide (n = 256) in uncontrolled diabetic patients with NYHA Class II and III heart failure plus an ejection fraction = 3 times the upper limit of normal. During all clinical studies in the U.S., 14 of 4780 (0.3%) pioglitazone-treated patients had ALT values > 3 times the upper limit of normal. After pioglitazone discontinuation, all patients with follow-up lab values had reversible elevations in ALT. In the population of patients treated with pioglitazone, mean values for bilirubin, AST, ALT, alkaline phosphatase, and GGT were decreased at the final visit compared with baseline. Fewer than 0.09% of pioglitazone-treated patients were withdrawn from clinical trials in the U.S. due to elevated hepatic enzymes. In pre-marketing clinical trials, there were no cases of drug-related liver failure. However, post-marketing cases of hepatitis and elevated hepatic enzymes (>= 3 times the upper limit of normal) have been reported during pioglitazone therapy. Very rarely, these reports have involved hepatic failure with and without fatal outcome. A published case report describes liver injury after 6 months of pioglitazone therapy (4 months at 15 mg/day followed by 2 months at 30 mg/day and one week of 45 mg/day) in a 49-year old man. The patient complained of anorexia, nausea/vomiting, and abdominal pain seven weeks prior to presentation; liver enzymes were normal and pioglitazone was continued. One week after a dose increase to 45 mg/day, scleral icterus, alcoholic stools, hyperbilirubinemia, and AST, ALT and alkaline phosphatase elevations were noted. A biopsy indicated mixed-hepatocellular-cholestatic liver injury. Pioglitazone was discontinued and liver enzymes returned to normal after six weeks. Pioglitazone is structurally related to troglitazone, a thiazolidinedione no longer marketed in the US, which has been was associated with idiosyncratic hepatotoxicity and rare cases of hepatic failure, hepatic transplants, and death during post-marketing clinical use. Elevated hepatic enzymes occur rarely with glimepiride monotherapy, and isolated cases of cholestasis, jaundice, and hepatitis have been infrequently reported. Other gastrointestinal effects that have occurred in patients treated with glimepiride in clinical trials include gastrointestinal pain (abdominal pain), nausea/vomiting (1.1%) and diarrhea. The incidence of diarrhea in patients taking pioglitazone in combination with a sulfonylurea is 4.3—6%. It is recommended that patients treated with glimepiride; pioglitazone undergo periodic monitoring of hepatic enzymes.Patients receiving glimepiride; pioglitazone therapy should report changes in vision to their health care provider. Blurred vision and/or changes in accommodation can occur with glimepiride therapy. These changes can be due to low or high blood glucose concentrations, or simply changes in blood glucose and can increase or become pronounced during the initiation of therapy. In placebo-controlled trials of glimepiride, the incidence of blurred vision was 0.4% for glimepiride vs. 0.7% placebo. In addition, new onset or worsening (diabetic) macular edema with visual impairment (decreased visual acuity) has been rarely reported during post-marketing experience with pioglitazone. Frequently, patients also reported peripheral edema. In some cases, symptoms improved or resolved following discontinuation of pioglitazone. Macular edema typically occurs in association with diabetic retinopathy, although it is more likely to occur as retinopathy progresses. Risk factors for macular edema include duration of diabetes, presence of retinopathy, hypertension, and poor glycemic control. Symptoms suggestive of macular edema include blurred vision or distorted vision, decreased color sensitivity, and decreased dark adaptation. Patients with diabetes should have regular eye exams by an ophthalmologist per the Standards of Care of the American Diabetes Association. Additionally, any diabetic who reports any kind of visual symptom should be promptly referred to an ophthalmologist, regardless of the patient's underlying medications or other physical findings.Changes in hematologic parameters may occur when patients are receiving glimepiride; pioglitazone. Decreases in hemoglobin concentrations of approximately 2—4% occur in patients treated with pioglitazone. The changes occur primarily during the first 4 to 12 weeks of therapy and remain relatively constant thereafter. These changes may be related to the increased plasma volume observed with pioglitazone therapy and have not been associated with any significant hematologic clinical effects. In U.S. double-blind studies, anemia was reported for 1% of pioglitazone-treated patients and 0% of placebo-treated patients in monotherapy studies. Anemia was reported for 1.6% of pioglitazone-treated patients and 1.6% of placebo-treated patients in combination with insulin. Anemia was reported for 0.3% of pioglitazone-treated patients and 1.6% of placebo-treated patients in combination with sulfonylurea. Anemia was reported for 1.2% of pioglitazone-treated patients and 0% of placebo-treated patients in combination with metformin. In addition, leukopenia, agranulocytosis, thrombocytopenia, hemolytic anemia, aplastic anemia, and pancytopenia have been reported with sulfonylureas, including glimepiride. The incidence of these effects with glimepiride is not known.Adverse dermatological reactions have been reported in fewer than 1% of patients treated with glimepiride. These include pruritus, erythema, urticaria, morbilliform or maculopapular rash. These effects can be transient despite continued use of glimepiride. If dermatologic reactions persist, glimepiride; pioglitazone therapy should be discontinued. Porphyria cutanea tarda and photosensitivity have been reported with sulfonylureas. Disulfiram reactions and hepatic porphyria reactions have been reported with sulfonylureas.Hyponatremia and SIADH are possible adverse effects when patients are treated with glimepiride; pioglitazone. Cases of hyponatremia have been reported with all sulfonylureas including glimepiride. The incidence appears to be greater in patients on other medications or with other diseases that cause hyponatremia or antidiuretic hormone secretion. SIADH secretion has been reported in patients taking sulfonylureas, including glimepiride. It has been suggested that certain sulfonylureas may augment the antidiuretic action of ADH and/or increase its release.Other adverse events that are possible with glimepiride; pioglitazone combination therapy will be similar to those reported when each drug is taken separately. Other adverse events that have been reported in placebo-controlled trials of glimepiride and in more than 1% of patients include dizziness (1.7% vs. 0.3% placebo), asthenia (1.6% vs. 1%), and headache (1.5% vs. 1.4%). Other frequently reported adverse reactions during placebo-controlled clinical trials of pioglitazone (>= 5% incidence) include diabetes mellitus aggravated, headache, hyperglycemia, myalgia, pharyngitis, sinusitis, tooth disorder, and upper respiratory tract infection. The incidence of withdrawals from clinical trials due to an adverse event other than hyperglycemia was similar for patients receiving placebo or pioglitazone.Data from the manufacturer's clinical trial database have identified an increase in the rate of bone fractures in women taking pioglitazone as compared to women taking a control (placebo or other antidiabetic agents); an increased rate of bone fractures has not been identified in men. The majority of fractures have occurred in the distal upper limb (forearm, hand, and wrist) or lower limb (foot, ankle, fibula, and tibia). In the PROactive trial, the rate of bone fracture after a mean follow-up of 34.5 months was 5.1% in female patients taking pioglitazone vs. 2.5% in female patients taking placebo; the different was noted after the first year of treatment and remained during the course of the study. An increased rate of fracture has also been identified in a large meta-analysis of 10 randomized, controlled trials involving 13,715 patients, and two observational studies involving 31,679 patients. Rosiglitazone and pioglitazone were associated with a significantly increased risk of fractures overall in the 10 randomized controlled trials (OR 1.45, 95% CI 1.18—1.79, p < 0.001); five trials showed a significantly increased risk of fractures among women (OR 2.23, 95% CI 1.65—3.01, p < 0.001) but not among men (OR 1.0, 95% CI 0.73—1.39, p = 0.98). Data from two randomized trials indicated that bone mineral density in women exposed to thiazolidinediones was significantly reduced at the lumbar spine (weighted mean difference -1.11%, 95% CI -2.08% to -0.14%, p = 0.02) and hip (weighted mean difference -1.24%, 95%CI -2.34% to -0.67%, p < 0.001). An explanation for the mechanism of bone fracture is not currently available. However, investigations into these findings are ongoing. The risk of fracture should be evaluated in female patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus who are currently being treated with glimepiride; pioglitazone, or when initiation of glimepiride; pioglitazone treatment is being considered.During required laboratory testing in clinical trials of pioglitazone, sporadic, transient elevations in creatine phosphokinase levels (CPK) were observed. In 9 patients, a single, isolated elevation to greater than 10 times the upper limit of normal (values of 2150 to 11,400) was noted. Six of these patients continued to receive pioglitazone, two patients had completed receiving study medication at the time of the elevated value, and one patient discontinued study medication due to the elevation. These elevations resolved without any apparent clinical sequelae. The relationship of these events to pioglitazone or glimepiride; pioglitazone therapy is unknown.In two 3-year studies in which pioglitazone was compared to placebo or glyburide, there were 16/3656 (0.44%) reports of bladder cancer in patients taking pioglitazone compared to 5/3679 (0.14%) in patients not taking pioglitazone. After excluding patients in whom exposure to study drug was less than one year at the time of diagnosis of bladder cancer, there were six (0.16%) cases on pioglitazone and two (0.05%) on placebo. Such effects are also possible when patients take pioglitazone; glimepiride.
  Adverse Reactions
    Headline
    asthenia, dizziness, headache, infection, myalgia, pharyngitis, sinusitis
    General Information
    Adverse events that are possible with pioglitazone; glimepiride combination therapy will be similar to those reported when each drug is taken separately. Adverse events reported during controlled 16-week clinical studies comparing placebo plus a sulfonylurea to pioglitazone plus a sulfonylurea were upper respiratory tract infection (15.5% and 16.6%) and accidental injury (8.6% and 3.5%). In a 24-week study of patients taking combination therapy with pioglitazone and a sulfonylurea, headache (4% to 7.1%), pain in limb (4% to 5.4%), upper respiratory tract infection (12.3% to 14.8%), and urinary tract infection (5.7% to 6.8%) were reported. Other adverse events reported in more than 1% of patients during placebo-controlled trials of glimepiride include dizziness (1.7% vs. 0.3% placebo), asthenia (1.6% vs. 1%), and headache (1.5% vs. 1.4%). Other frequently reported adverse reactions during placebo-controlled clinical trials of pioglitazone (5% or greater incidence) include diabetes mellitus aggravated, headache (9.1% vs. 6.9%), myalgia (5.4% vs. 2.7%), pharyngitis (5.1% vs. 0.8%), sinusitis (6.3% vs. 4.6%), tooth disorder (5.3%), and upper respiratory tract infection (13.2% vs. 8.5%).

    Headline
    hypoglycemia
    General Information
    Hypoglycemia, which can be severe, can occur during pioglitazone; glimepiride therapy. During clinical trials, the incidence of hypoglycemia with pioglitazone (30 or 45 mg/day) in combination with a sulfonylurea was 13.4% to 15.7%. Hypoglycemia is the most common side effect of therapy with sulfonylureas. The incidence of severe hypoglycemia with sulfonylurea therapy is estimated as high as 7% per year, and has been reported as low as roughly 1.8%. Mild episodes are likely to occur even more frequently and have been self-reported in clinical practice to be as high as 39%. Hypoglycemia can be a result of excessive dosage, but it also could be due to other factors such as improper diet or excessive physical activity. Hypoglycemia symptoms may include hunger, pallor, nausea, fatigue, diaphoresis, headache, palpitations, numbness of the mouth, tingling in the fingers, tremor, muscle weakness, visual changes, hypothermia, uncontrolled yawning, irritability, mental confusion, sinus tachycardia, shallow breathing, or loss of consciousness. Prolonged hypoglycemia can result in irreversible brain damage and even death.

    Headline
    dyspnea, edema, heart failure, peripheral edema
    General Information
    In all U.S. clinical trials, edema, including peripheral edema, was reported more frequently in pioglitazone-treated patients (1.6% to 26.7%) than placebo-treated patients (1.2% to 15.3%), and similar rates are expected with pioglitazone; glimepiride combination treatment. In monotherapy studies, edema was reported for 4.8% of pioglitazone-treated patients versus 1.2% of placebo-treated patients. In a 16-week trial of patients receiving placebo plus a sulfonylurea or pioglitazone plus a sulfonylurea, the incidence of combined edema/peripheral edema was 2.1% and 7.2%, respectively. In addition, in a 24-week trial, lower limb edema (5.7% to 12.3%) was reported in patients receiving pioglitazone plus a sulfonylurea. Pioglitazone-induced edema is reversible upon discontinuation of pioglitazone therapy. Postmarketing cases of congestive heart failure have been reported in patients receiving pioglitazone (patients with and without a prior history of heart disease). In the PROactive study, serious heart failure occurred in 5.7% of patients with type 2 diabetes and a previous history of macrovascular disease receiving pioglitazone (n = 2605) vs. 4.1% of patients receiving placebo (n = 2633). Death secondary to heart failure was not different between the 2 groups. Furthermore, in a 24-week postmarketing safety study, pioglitazone (n = 262) was compared to glyburide (n = 256) in uncontrolled diabetic patients with NYHA Class II and III heart failure plus an ejection fraction less than 40%. Overnight hospitalization for congestive heart failure was higher in the pioglitazone group when compared to the glyburide group (9.9% vs. 4.7%, respectively); the risk of congestive heart failure is increased in patients taking insulin in combination with pioglitazone. In a 16-week clinical trial of combined insulin plus pioglitazone therapy, more patients developed congestive heart failure on combination therapy (1.1%, 4 patients) compared to 0% receiving insulin monotherapy; all patients who developed heart failure had a history of cardiac disease (e.g., coronary artery disease, prior CABG, myocardial infarction). No difference in cardiovascular mortality was observed. Patients receiving pioglitazone; glimepiride should be monitored for dyspnea or changes in weight; if unusually rapid weight increase occurs, evaluate for fluid accumulation and volume-related adverse effects, such as heart failure or edema. Pioglitazone; glimepiride should be discontinued if heart failure occurs or if cardiac status deteriorates during therapy.

    Headline
    fluid retention, weight gain
    General Information
    During clinical trials, treatment with pioglitazone has been associated with weight gain (9.1% to 13.4% of patients taking pioglitazone plus a sulfonylurea). Weight gain has also been reported more frequently in patients treated with pioglitazone monotherapy (2.7% to 13.9%) than in placebo-treated patients (0.5%). During clinical trials, the mean weight gain in patients treated with pioglitazone was 0.9 kg (15 mg/day), 1 kg (30 mg/day), and 2.6 kg (45 mg/day) when given as monotherapy and 0.9 kg (30 mg/day) and 1.8 kg (45 mg/day) when administered in combination with metformin. Similarly, the mean weight gain was 2.3 kg (15 mg/day), 3.3 kg (30 mg/day), and 4.1 kg (45 mg/day) when administered in combination with insulin and 2 kg (15 mg/day), 3.3 kg (30 mg/day), and 4.1 kg (45 mg/day) when administered in combination with a sulfonylurea. The mean weight change in the control arms of these studies was -1.4 kg in the placebo patients, -1.4 kg in the metformin only patients, 0.2 kg in the insulin only patients, and -0.5 kg in the insulin plus sulfonylurea patients. The mechanism of weight gain is not fully understood but is thought to be due to fluid retention and fat accumulation. Patients receiving pioglitazone; glimepiride should be monitored for changes in weight; if unusually rapid weight gain occurs, evaluate for fluid accumulation and volume-related adverse effects, such as heart failure or edema.

    Headline
    abdominal pain, anorexia, cholestasis, diarrhea, elevated hepatic enzymes, hepatic failure, hepatitis, hyperbilirubinemia, jaundice, nausea, vomiting
    General Information
    Common gastrointestinal effects reported with the use of pioglitazone and a sulfonylurea (e.g., pioglitazone; glimepiride therapy) include diarrhea (4.3% to 6%) and nausea (4% to 5.1%). Gastrointestinal effects that have occurred in patients treated with glimepiride in clinical trials include gastrointestinal pain (abdominal pain) (less than 1%), nausea (1.1%), vomiting (less than 1%), and diarrhea (less than 1%). Pioglitazone is structurally related to troglitazone, a thiazolidinedione no longer marketed in the U.S., that was associated with idiosyncratic hepatotoxicity. During placebo-controlled clinical trials in the U.S., a total of 4 of 1,526 (0.26%) pioglitazone-treated patients and 2 of 793 (0.25%) placebo-treated patients had ALT values 3 times the upper limit of normal (ULN) or greater. During all clinical studies in the U.S., 14 of 4,780 (0.3%) pioglitazone-treated patients had ALT values greater than 3 times the ULN. After pioglitazone discontinuation, all patients with follow-up lab values had reversible elevations in ALT. In the population of patients treated with pioglitazone, mean values for bilirubin, AST, ALT, alkaline phosphatase, and GGT were decreased at the final visit compared with baseline. Fewer than 0.09% of pioglitazone-treated patients were withdrawn from clinical trials in the U.S. due to elevated hepatic enzymes. In premarketing clinical trials, there were no cases of drug-related liver failure. However, postmarketing cases of hepatitis and elevated hepatic enzymes (3 times the ULN or greater) have been reported during pioglitazone therapy. Very rarely, these reports have involved hepatic failure with and without fatal outcome. A published case report describes liver injury after 6 months of pioglitazone therapy (4 months at 15 mg/day followed by 2 months at 30 mg/day and one week of 45 mg/day) in a 49-year old man. The patient complained of anorexia, nausea, vomiting, and abdominal pain 7 weeks prior to presentation; liver enzymes were normal and pioglitazone was continued. One week after a dose increase to 45 mg/day, scleral icterus, alcoholic stools, hyperbilirubinemia, and AST, ALT, and alkaline phosphatase elevations were noted. A biopsy indicated mixed-hepatocellular-cholestatic liver injury. Pioglitazone was discontinued and liver enzymes returned to normal after 6 weeks. Elevated hepatic enzymes rarely occur with glimepiride monotherapy, and isolated cases of cholestasis, jaundice, and hepatitis have been infrequently reported. It is recommended that patients treated with pioglitazone; glimepiride undergo periodic monitoring of hepatic enzymes.

    Headline
    blurred vision, macular edema, visual impairment
    General Information
    Patients receiving pioglitazone; glimepiride therapy should report changes in vision to their health care provider. Blurred vision and/or changes in accommodation can occur with glimepiride therapy. These changes can be due to low or high blood glucose concentrations, or simply changes in blood glucose, and can increase or become pronounced during the initiation of therapy. In placebo-controlled trials of glimepiride, the incidence of blurred vision was 0.4% for glimepiride vs. 0.7% placebo. In addition, new onset or worsening (diabetic) macular edema with visual impairment (decreased visual acuity) has rarely been reported during postmarketing experience with pioglitazone. Frequently, patients also reported peripheral edema. In some cases, symptoms improved or resolved following discontinuation of pioglitazone. Macular edema typically occurs in association with diabetic retinopathy, although it is more likely to occur as retinopathy progresses. Risk factors for macular edema include duration of diabetes, presence of retinopathy, hypertension, and poor glycemic control. Symptoms suggestive of macular edema include blurred vision or distorted vision, decreased color sensitivity, and decreased dark adaptation. Patients with diabetes should have regular eye exams by an ophthalmologist per the Standards of Care of the American Diabetes Association. Additionally, any diabetic who reports any kind of visual symptom should be promptly referred to an ophthalmologist, regardless of the patient's underlying medications or other physical findings.

    Headline
    agranulocytosis, anemia, aplastic anemia, hemolytic anemia, leukopenia, pancytopenia, thrombocytopenia
    General Information
    Changes in hematologic parameters may occur when patients are receiving pioglitazone; glimepiride. Decreases in hemoglobin concentrations of approximately 2% to 4% occur in patients treated with pioglitazone. The changes occur primarily during the first 4 to 12 weeks of therapy and remain relatively constant thereafter. These changes may be related to the increased plasma volume observed with pioglitazone therapy and have not been associated with any significant hematologic clinical effects. In U.S. double-blind studies, anemia was reported in 2% or less of patients treated with pioglitazone plus a sulfonylurea. In addition, leukopenia, agranulocytosis, thrombocytopenia, hemolytic anemia, aplastic anemia, and pancytopenia have been reported with sulfonylureas, including glimepiride. The incidence of these effects with glimepiride is not known.

    alopecia, anaphylactoid reactions, angioedema, erythema, flushing, maculopapular rash, photosensitivity, porphyria, pruritus, Stevens-Johnson syndrome, urticaria
    General Information
    Adverse dermatological reactions have been reported in fewer than 1% of patients treated with glimepiride; similar incidences are expected with pioglitazone; glimepiride therapy. These include pruritus, erythema, urticaria, morbilliform rash, or maculopapular rash. Cutaneous eruptions with or without pruritus, anaphylactoid reactions, angioedema, alopecia, and Stevens-Johnson Syndrome have been reported during postmarketing experience with glimepiride. These effects can be transient despite continued use of glimepiride. If dermatologic reactions persist, pioglitazone; glimepiride therapy should be discontinued. Porphyria cutanea tarda and photosensitivity have been reported with sulfonylureas. Other reactions rarely (less than 0.1%) reported with sulfonylureas include disulfiram-like reactions when taken with alcohol and these reactions are associated with flushing of the skin; if this reaction occurs, it may also be associated with rare abdominal cramps, nausea, and headache. Hepatic porphyria has been reported with sulfonylureas, including glimepiride.

    Headline
    hyponatremia, SIADH
    General Information
    Hyponatremia and SIADH are possible adverse effects when patients are treated with pioglitazone; glimepiride. Cases of hyponatremia have been reported with all sulfonylureas including glimepiride. The incidence appears to be greater in patients on other medications or with other diseases that cause hyponatremia or antidiuretic hormone secretion. SIADH secretion has been reported in patients taking sulfonylureas, including glimepiride. It has been suggested that certain sulfonylureas may augment the antidiuretic action of ADH and/or increase its release.

    Headline
    bone fractures, osteopenia
    General Information
    Data from the manufacturer's clinical trial database have identified an increase in the rate of bone fractures in women taking pioglitazone as compared to women taking a control (placebo or other antidiabetic agents); an increased rate of bone fractures has not been identified in men. The majority of fractures have occurred in the distal upper limb (forearm, hand, and wrist) or lower limb (foot, ankle, fibula, and tibia). In the PROactive trial, the rate of bone fracture after a mean follow-up of 34.5 months was 5.1% in female patients taking pioglitazone vs. 2.5% in female patients taking placebo; the difference was noted after the first year of treatment and remained during the course of the study. An increased rate of fracture has also been identified in a large meta-analysis of 10 randomized, controlled trials involving 13,715 patients, and 2 observational studies involving 31,679 patients. Rosiglitazone and pioglitazone were associated with a significantly increased risk of fractures overall in the 10 randomized controlled trials (OR 1.45, 95% CI 1.18 to 1.79, p less than 0.001); of these, 5 trials showed a significantly increased risk of fractures among women (OR 2.23, 95% CI 1.65 to 3.01, p less than 0.001) but not among men (OR 1.0, 95% CI 0.73 to 1.39, p = 0.98). Osteopenia may be increased in females taking these medications. Data from 2 randomized trials indicated that bone mineral density in women exposed to thiazolidinediones was significantly reduced at the lumbar spine (weighted mean difference -1.11%, 95% CI -2.08% to -0.14%, p = 0.02) and hip (weighted mean difference -1.24%, 95%CI -2.34% to -0.67%, p less than 0.001). An explanation for the mechanism of bone fracture is not currently available. However, investigations into these findings are ongoing. The risk of fracture should be evaluated in female patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus who are currently being treated with pioglitazone; glimepiride, or when initiation of pioglitazone; glimepiride treatment is being considered.

    Headline
    new primary malignancy
    General Information
    Pioglitazone may increase the risk for new primary malignancy, specifically bladder cancer. Pioglitazone; glimepiride should not be used in patients with bladder cancer. In patients with a prior history of bladder cancer, the benefits of blood sugar control should be weighed against the unknown risks for cancer recurrence. In December 2016, the FDA concluded that use of pioglitazone may be linked to an increased risk of bladder cancer; this was based on an extensive evidence review. Patients currently taking pioglitazone; glimepiride are encouraged to report any symptoms of bladder cancer to their health care provider. Symptoms of bladder cancer include blood or red-colored urine (hematuria), new or worsening urge to urinate, and pain when urinating. Both patients and health care providers should report pioglitazone; glimepiride related adverse events to the FDA's MedWatch Safety Information and Adverse Events Reporting Program. In September 2010 and June 2011, the FDA warned about the possible risk of bladder cancer with pioglitazone based on interim results from a 10-year U.S. epidemiologic study and a French epidemiological study. Based on the results of the French study, France has suspended the use of pioglitazone and Germany has recommended not to start pioglitazone in new patients. The FDA has reviewed additional published studies evaluating the risk of bladder cancer in patients treated with pioglitazone, including the final 10-year results of the U.S. epidemiologic study. The results did not show an increased risk for bladder cancer in patients who used pioglitazone at some point (ever users) compared to patients who never used pioglitazone (never users), with a fully adjusted HR of 1.06 (95% CI 0.89 to 1.26). The investigators identified 1,075 newly diagnosed cases of bladder cancer in never users (n = 158,918) and 186 cases in ever users (n = 34,181). The study also suggested a modest trend towards higher risk with increasing duration of use, but this trend was not statistically significant; it could have been due to chance. Compared to the interim 5-year results, these final 10-year results found weaker associations that were not statistically significant. However, the directions of the associations remained unchanged. In addition, during the 3 year PROactive Study (PROspective pioglitAzone Clinical Trial In macroVascular Events), 14 patients out of 2,605 (0.54%) randomized to pioglitazone and 5 out of 2,633 (0.19%) randomized to placebo were diagnosed with bladder cancer. After excluding patients in whom exposure to study drug was less than 1 year at the time of diagnosis of bladder cancer, there were 6 (0.23%) cases receiving pioglitazone and 2 (0.08%) cases receiving placebo. In a 10-year observational follow-up of the PROactive Study, researchers investigated the occurrence of cardiovascular outcomes and malignancies after the PROactive clinical trial period ended. Among patients who entered the follow-up study, the median total follow-up (clinical trial and observational periods) was 12.8 years. Results revealed the imbalance in the number of bladder cancer cases observed in patients using pioglitazone during the clinical trial period (RR 2.83; 95% CI 1.02 to 7.85) did not persist during the combined 12.8-year period (HR 1; 95% CI 0.59 to 1.72). In contrast, in a retrospective cohort study that assessed the association between pioglitazone use and bladder cancer, statistically significant trends in the risk of bladder cancer were observed with increasing cumulative duration of use and cumulative dose of pioglitazone. The investigators used the United Kingdom Clinical Practice Research Datalink (CPRD) to identify a cohort of 145,806 patients newly treated with antidiabetic drugs (n = 10,951 initiators of pioglitazone) with a mean follow-up of 4.7 years, during which 622 patients received a diagnosis of bladder cancer. Of these, 54 developed bladder cancer after pioglitazone exposure. The fully adjusted HR for bladder cancer with pioglitazone use compared with no thiazolidinedione use was 1.63 (95% CI 1.22 to 2.19).

    Headline
    elevated creatine kinase, rhabdomyolysis
    General Information
    The FDA has received reports of cases of rhabdomyolysis occurring during pioglitazone use; a causal relationship has not been determined. The FDA will continue to evaluate the potential safety risk associated with these cases. At this time, the FDA does not recommend any changes to the prescribing habits or use of pioglitazone; glimepiride or other pioglitazone containing products. During required laboratory testing in controlled clinical trials, sporadic, transient elevated creatine kinase concentrations (CK) were observed. In 9 patients (0.2%), a single, isolated elevation to greater than 10 times the upper limit of normal (ULN); CK values of 2,150 to 11,400 international units/L were noted. These elevations resolved without any apparent clinical sequelae. The relationship of these events to pioglitazone therapy is unknown. Patients who have questions about their use of pioglitazone should contact their health care provider. Health care providers are encouraged to report any adverse effects associated with pioglitazone to the FDA through the MedWatch adverse event reporting program.

    Headline
    menstrual irregularity
    General Information
    The frequency of reports relating to menstrual irregularity/dysfunction with thiazolidinediones in clinical trials was low (approximately 0.4%) and similar to placebo. If unexpected menstrual irregularity occurs, evaluate and consider the benefits of continued pioglitazone therapy. Thiazolidinediones, including pioglitazone in pioglitazone; glimepiride, may cause ovulation in premenopausal anovulatory females, such as those with polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS), resulting in unplanned pregnancy. Description
  Text
  Pioglitazone; glimepiride is a combination product containing 2 oral antidiabetic agents. Pioglitazone is a member of the thiazolidinedione (TZD) class. Glimepiride is a second-generation, long-acting sulfonylurea. The combination is used as an adjunct to diet and exercise to lower blood glucose concentrations in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM). The addition of pioglitazone to a sulfonylurea further reduces fasting plasma glucose and hemoglobin A1C vs. monotherapy; A1C reductions range from 0.88% to 1.67% depending on the dose of pioglitazone used. First-line T2DM therapy depends on comorbidities, individual-centered treatment factors, and management needs. In individuals with T2DM with or at high risk for atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD), heart failure (HF), and/or chronic kidney disease (CKD), certain glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonists (GLP-1 RAs) or sodium-glucose co-transporter 2 (SGLT2) inhibitors with proven benefit are appropriate initial therapy, with or without metformin based on glycemic needs. Pioglitazone should be considered to reduce the risk of stroke or myocardial infarction (MI) in persons with a history of stroke and evidence of insulin resistance, prediabetes, or T2DM. In individuals with T2DM who do not have ASCVD/indicators of high-risk, HF, or CKD, the TZDs, like pioglitazone, are associated with glycemic durability; however, the benefits must be balanced with the risk of adverse effects such as heart failure, edema, decreased bone mineral density, and weight gain. Sulfonylureas are generally considered to have equivalent efficacy in the treatment of T2DM with an expected 1% to 2% decrease in hemoglobin A1C. Disadvantages of sulfonylurea therapy include hypoglycemia (especially in the geriatric adult), weight gain, and frequent loss of efficacy over time; however, the weight gain is relatively modest in large cohort studies and the incidence of severe hypoglycemia is lower than with insulin. Pioglitazone; glimepiride combination products were initally FDA approved in 2006.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Pioglitazone; glimepiride combines 2 oral antidiabetic agents with different mechanisms to improve glycemic control in patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus (DM). Glimepiride acts primarily by increasing insulin secretion. Pioglitazone is an insulin sensitizer that acts by enhancing peripheral glucose utilization. Neither drug is effective in patients with insulin deficiency (i.e., type 1 DM).
Pioglitazone: Pioglitazone is an oral thiazolidinedione; its primary action is enhancement of insulin sensitivity in adipose tissue, skeletal muscle and the liver. Pioglitazone is a highly selective and potent agonist for the peroxisome proliferator activated receptor (PPAR-gamma), which regulates the transcription of certain insulin responsive genes. PPAR receptors can be found in key target tissues for insulin action such as adipose tissue, skeletal muscle, and the liver. Clinically, pioglitazone decreases plasma glucose concentrations, insulin concentrations, and glycosylated hemoglobin. Additional favorable metabolic effects include decreased hepatic glucose output, reduced free fatty acid serum concentrations, and improved lipid profiles. Unlike glimepiride, pioglitazone enhances tissue sensitivity to insulin rather than stimulates insulin secretion.
Glimepiride: Glimepiride lowers blood sugar by stimulating pancreatic islet cells, resulting in an increase in insulin secretion. Sulfonylureas are believed to bind to ATP-sensitive potassium-channel receptors on the pancreatic cell surface, thereby reducing potassium conductance and causing depolarization of the membrane. Depolarization stimulates calcium ion influx through voltage-sensitive calcium channels, raising intracellular concentrations of calcium ions, which induces the secretion, or exocytosis, of insulin. The drug is not effective in the absence of functioning beta-cells, as occurs in diabetes mellitus type 1, or when the number of viable beta-cells is low, as occurs in severe cases of diabetes mellitus type 2. Prolonged administration of sulfonylureas also produces extrapancreatic effects that contribute to their hypoglycemic activity. These effects include reduction of basal hepatic glucose production and an enhanced peripheral sensitivity to insulin secondary to an increase in insulin receptors or to changes in the events that follow insulin-receptor binding. The relative importance of each of these actions to the overall therapeutic effect of the drug will vary among oral antidiabetic agents and from patient to patient, which might account for the variability in potency among these drugs. Glimepiride may enhance peripheral tissue insulin sensitivity to a greater degree in fatty tissue than in skeletal muscle.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    The area under the curve (AUC) and maximum concentration (Cmax) of pioglitazone and glimepiride administered as separate tablets are bioequivalent to the respective doses of the combination tablets. Food delayed the peak serum concentration of pioglitazone by 1.6 to 3.6 hours and decreased the Cmax of pioglitazone by 9%; however, these changes are not expected to be clinically significant. Food did not change the peak concentration of glimepiride.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    Compared with healthy patients, patients with impaired hepatic function (Child-Pugh Grade B and C) have an approximate 45% reduction in pioglitazone peak concentrations with no change in AUC values. Pioglitazone should not be initiated in patients with clinical evidence of active liver disease or ALT valuesmore than 2.5 times the upper limit of normal at baselin. Glimepiride has not been studied in patients with active liver disease. As with other sulfonylureas, hepatic insufficiency may cause elevated drug levels of the sulfonylurea and diminish endogenous gluconeogenic capacity, both of which increase the risk of serious hypoglycemic reactions.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    The pharmacokinetics of pioglitazone is not changed in patients with renal insufficiency. The relative total clearance of glimepiride increased when kidney function was impaired (e.g., CrCl 10 to 60 mL/minute). The elimination of the 2 major glimepiride metabolites (M1 and M2) was reduced in patients with renal impairment. In an animal model, M1 has about 1/3 the pharmacological activity of glimepiride in animal models; M2 is not active. In a small single-dose study was administered to patients with mild renal impairment (Group I, CrCl more than 50 mL/minute), patients with moderate renal impairment (Group II, CrCl 20 to 50 mL/minute) and patients with severe renal impairment (Group III, CrCl less than 20 mL/minute). Although glimepiride serum concentrations decreased with decreasing renal function, Group III had a 2.3-fold higher mean AUC for M1 and an 8.6-fold higher mean AUC for M2 compared to the mean AUCs in Group I. The apparent terminal half-life for glimepiride did not change, while the half-lives for M1 and M2 increased as renal function decreased. Mean urinary excretion of M1 plus M2 as a percentage of dose decreased from 44.4% for Group I to 21.9% for Group II and 9.3% for Group III.
    Name
    Geriatric
    Description
    In elderly patients, the mean AUC of glimepiride at steady state is about 13% lower compared to younger patients, and the clearance is approximately 11% higher; age does not significantly affect the pharmacokinetics of pioglitazone.
    Name
    Gender Differences
    Description
    The mean Cmax and AUC values of pioglitazone are increased by 20% and 60%, respectively in females; however, adjustments in initial dosing based on gender alone are not necessary.
  Text
  Pioglitazone; glimepiride is administered orally.
Pioglitazone: Protein binding is extensive (greater than 99%), primarily to serum albumin. Pioglitazone is extensively metabolized by hydroxylation and oxidation. It is primarily metabolized by CYP2C8 and to a lesser extent, CYP3A4 with contributions from a variety of other isoforms including the mainly extrahepatic CYP1A1 enzyme. In animal models of type 2 diabetes, metabolites M2 and M4 (hydroxy derivatives of pioglitazone) and M3 (keto derivative of pioglitazone) are pharmacologically active. Metabolites M3 and M4 are the principal drug-related species found in human serum following multiple dosing. At steady state, serum concentrations of metabolites M3 and M4 are equal to or greater than serum concentrations of pioglitazone. In both healthy volunteers and in patients with type 2 diabetes, pioglitazone comprises approximately 30% to 50% of the total peak serum concentrations and 20% to 25% of the total AUC at steady state. Approximately 15% to 30% of the total pioglitazone dose is recovered in the urine. Renal elimination of pioglitazone is negligible, and the drug is excreted primarily as metabolites and their conjugates. Most of an oral dose is presumed to be excreted into the bile either unchanged or as metabolites and eliminated in the feces. The mean serum half-lives of pioglitazone and its metabolites is 3 to 7 hours and 16 to 24 hours, respectively.
Glimepiride: The pharmacokinetics of glimepiride do not differ between healthy subjects and patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus. It is greater than 99.5% protein-bound. Glimepiride is completely oxidatively metabolized after administration. Two metabolites are formed, the cyclohexyl hydroxy methyl derivative (M1) and the carboxyl derivative (M2). The hepatic isoenzyme CYP2C9 is involved in the biotransformation to M1. M1 is metabolized to M2 via an unknown pathway. M2 is not active. In an animal model, M1 has about 1/3 the pharmacological activity of glimepiride; the clinical significance of M1 on glucose-lowering effects is not clear. Glimepiride is excreted in the urine (60%) and feces (40%), predominantly in the form of M1 and M2. The metabolites M1 and M2 accounted for about 70% (ratio of M1 to M2 was 1:3) of the dose recovered in feces. No parent drug was recovered from urine or feces. The half-life of glimepiride is 5 hours initially but increases to roughly 9 hours after multiple dosing. Glimepiride does not accumulate in serum following multiple dosing. Clearance does not change over the 1 mg to 8 mg dose range, indicating linear pharmacokinetics.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 (CYP450) isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP2C9, CYP2C8, CYP3A4
Pioglitazone: Pioglitazone is primarily metabolized by CYP2C8 and to a lesser extent, CYP3A4 with contributions from a variety of other isoforms including the mainly extrahepatic CYP1A1 enzyme. Clinically significant interactions may occur when administered with drugs that inhibit or induce CYP2C8. If given with a strong CYP2C8 inhibitor, do not exceed pioglitazone 15 mg daily. Urinary 6-beta-hydroxycortisol/cortisol ratios measured in patients treated with the drug showed that pioglitazone is not a strong CYP3A4 enzyme inducer.
Glimepiride: Sulfonylureas are substrates of the CYP2C9 isoenzyme; thus, coadministration with CYP2C9 inhibitors or inducers will increase or decrease, respectively, sulfonylurea concentrations.

    Name
    Oral Administration
    Description
    Administer once daily with the first meal of the day.

    Name

    Name

    Name

    Name

    Max Dosage

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Avoid use of pioglitazone; glimepiride in patients with active hepatic disease or if ALT more than 2.5 times the upper limit of normal.
  Renal Impairment
  In patients with renal impairment, glimepiride 1 mg/day PO should be initiated before pioglitazone; glimepiride (Duetact). If glimepiride is tolerated, the patient can be switched to combination therapy. The initial dose and dosage increments should be conservative to avoid hypoglycemia.
